<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every regular language over a finite alphabet has generalized star height at most three, with complement taken in the same free monoid. We express finite-monoid computations using a prefix code of word pieces.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ and every regular language $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$, $$h_\Sigma(L)\le3.$$
  - Explanation: Complement permits a universal upper bound on nesting for every regular language over its original finite alphabet.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-alg-split)): Let $D'\subseteq D\subseteq E$, where $E$ is a prefix code, and give each $e\in D$ a deterministic total update $G_e$ of a finite set $S$. Suppose languages $P_x,Q_y\subseteq\Sigma^*$, $x,y\in S$, satisfy:* 1. *If $w\in D^*$ has a prefix $uv$ with $u\in P_x$ and $v\in Q_y$, then $w$ has a first episode $e$, $uv=e$, and $G_e(x)=y$.* 2. *For every $e\in D\setminus D'$ and every $x\in S$, $e\in P_xQ_{G_e(x)}$.* *Let $W_{x,y}$ be the graph languages for these same updates on $(D')^*$. Then the graph language from $x$ to $y$ on $D^*$ is $$\label{alg:split-formula} \begin{split} D^*\cap\Bigg[W_{x,y}\ \cup\ & \left(\bigcup_{i\in S}W_{x,i}P_i\right) \left(\bigcup_{j,i\in S}Q_jW_{j,i}P_i\right)^*\\[-2pt] &\hspace{24mm}\cdot \left(\bigcup_{l\in S}Q_lW_{l,y}\right)\Bigg]. \end{split}$$ If $D$, all $P_x$, and all $Q_y$ have height at most one, while all $W_{x,y}$ have height at most $H\ge0$, the resulting graph languages have height at most $\max\{2,1+H\}$.
  - Explanation: Independent prefix and suffix tests propagate deterministic episode updates when soundness, exact consumption and completeness outside the skipped domain hold.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-alg-affine-recovery)): Let $S$ be a finite vector space and let the episode updates on $D\subseteq E$ be $$G_e(v)=vL_e+b_e,$$ with $L_e\in\operatorname{End}_{\rm lin}(S)$ and $b_e\in S$ determined by $e$. If $R_{x,z}$ are the graph languages of their composites on $D^*$, then the graph language for the product of their linear parts, from $x$ to $y$, is $$\label{alg:linear-recovery-formula} \bigcup_{b\in S} \bigl(R_{x,y+b}\cap R_{0,b}\bigr).$$ A common height bound for the $R_{x,z}$ is preserved.
  - Explanation: Outputs on the same word at the input and at zero recover the product of linear parts by finite Boolean operations.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift)): For a finite vector space $S$ over a finite field $\mathbf F$, the map $F(v)=vL+b$ has the linear lift on $S\oplus\mathbf F$ $$\label{alg:lift-formula} \widehat F(v,c)=(vL+cb,c).$$ Products of these lifts are the lifts of the corresponding composites in reading order. In particular, graph languages for products of the lifts give those of the affine composites by using inputs $(v,1)$ and outputs $(y,1)$, without increasing height.
  - Explanation: An additional homogeneous coordinate turns affine composition into linear composition in reading order.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-alg-shift-table)): For every nonempty finite set $A$ and every finite vector space $S$ there is an integer $l\ge1$, depending only on $\|A\|$ and $\|S\|$, with the following property. For every function $$f:A^l\longrightarrow \operatorname{End}_{\rm lin}(S)$$ there is a function $\beta_f:A^l\to S$ such that, for every $\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_l)\in A^l$ and $x\in S$, there is an $i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}$ for which $$\label{alg:shift-constancy} b\longmapsto x f(a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1},b,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_l) +\beta_f(a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1},b,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_l)$$ is constant on $A$. The integer $l$ is uniform over $f$, and $\beta_f$ is independent of $x$.
  - Explanation: A correction fixed independently of the input allows a tuple and input to select a coordinate line with constant corrected output.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-clock-robust)): Let $\Lambda$ and $\mathcal P$ be finite nonempty sets, with $m=\|\Lambda\|$. There exist a finite-dimensional torus $\mathcal T$, sets $I_\alpha,J_\alpha\subseteq\mathcal T$ for $\alpha\in\Lambda$, a set $W\subseteq\mathcal T$, points $v_p\in\mathcal T$ for $p\in\mathcal P$, and constants $\rho,\eta>0$ with these properties:* 1. *For each $z\in\mathcal T$, the edge set $$\label{clock:edge-set} \mathcal E_z=\{(\alpha,\lambda)\in\Lambda^2: z\in I_\alpha+J_\lambda\}$$ is either contained in the diagonal or consists of one off-diagonal pair.* 2. *If $z\notin W$, then for every $\alpha\in\Lambda$, $I_\alpha\cap(z-J_\alpha)$ contains a ball of radius $\rho$. In particular $\mathcal E_z$ is the full diagonal.* 3. *For every family $(\tilde v_p)_{p\in\mathcal P}$ satisfying $\operatorname{dist}(\tilde v_p,v_p)<\eta$ for all $p$, and every $a\in\mathcal T$, $$\label{clock:roster-estimate} \#\{(p,p')\in\mathcal P^2:p\ne p',\quad a+\tilde v_{p'}-\tilde v_p\in W\} \le c_{\rm cl}\log(2m), \qquad c_{\rm cl}=10^7.$$* *The constants $\rho,\eta$ and the dimension may depend on both finite sets, but $c_{\rm cl}$ is absolute.
  - Explanation: A finite torus clock limits independently chosen unequal tags and bounds exceptional roster pairs uniformly under translation and small perturbations.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-marker-local)): Fix a nonempty finite alphabet $\Sigma$ and integers $d\ge2$ and $K>3d^2$. There are an integer $r\ge K$ and a fixed rule assigning marker positions to every two-sided word in $\Sigma^{\mathbb Z}$ such that:* 1. *distinct markers have distance at least $d$;* 2. *the decision at $x$ uses only the letters on $[x-r,x+r)$, and the rule commutes with all integer translations;* 3. *if $[z-d,z+d]$ contains no marker, the block $[z,z+K)$ has a positive period less than $d$.
  - Explanation: A local translation invariant marker rule separates markers and forces short periodic blocks in markerless neighborhoods.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-marker-periodic)): Two two-sided words with respective positive periods $a,b$ that agree on $ab$ consecutive letter positions agree everywhere. Consequently, for $d\ge2$ and $K>3d^2$:* 1. *a length-$K$ block having a period below $d$ determines a unique two-sided periodic continuation with some period below $d$;* 2. *any finite nonempty family of such blocks in one word, whose start positions have diameter $w$ with $K-w\ge d^2$, determines one common continuation, agreeing with the word on their union;* 3. *for the rule in Lemma <a href="#marker:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="marker:local">7</a>, if the inclusive interval $[z,y]$ contains no marker and $y-z\ge2d$, the word agrees with one two-sided word of period less than $d$ throughout $$\label{marker:markerless-run} [z+d,y-d+K).$$* *The same assertions apply to a finite word when all stated blocks and marker inspection windows are present in that word.
  - Explanation: Sufficient overlap identifies periodic continuations and joins markerless blocks into a common periodic run.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-schedule-inner-cuts)): For $1\le j\le g$, put $c_j=(j+\tfrac12)G$. The main cuts of block $j$ are $$\label{schedule:main-cuts} k=t+c_j+aB,\qquad 0\le a<R.$$ At any such cut, all inner searches through index $j-1$, including their marker inspections, lie before $k$. The searches from index $j$ onward lie after $k$ even when performed at any of the hypothesized origins $k-c_j-a'B$, $0\le a'<R$, and with any width index $u$.* *For $0\le j\le g$ and $0\le u<B$, the periodic cuts are $$\label{schedule:periodic-cut-formula} k=z_j(t)+H_u+L_{\rm lag}+aB, \qquad 0\le a<ndN_M.$$ The whole search for $f_j^u(t)$, with its inspections, lies before every one of these cuts. Every offset $k-t$ in either schedule is divisible by $B$.
  - Explanation: Fixed cut schedules put earlier searches on the prefix side and later searches on the suffix side under every prescribed hypothesis.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-schedule-periodic-cuts)): Fix $j,u$ and an origin $t$. Suppose the interval $[z,z+H_u+H_{\rm ext}]$, where $z=z_j(t)$, contains no inner marker and all required inspections are available. There is a period $d'<d$ such that, with $k_0=z+H_u+L_{\rm lag}$, the cuts $$\label{schedule:periodic-progression} k=k_0+a d'N_MB,\qquad 0\le a<n,$$ belong to <a href="#schedule:periodic-cut-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="schedule:periodic-cut-formula">[schedule:periodic-cut-formula]</a>. For a fixed beginning $s\le z$, $T_{s,k}$ is constant along this progression. Moreover $\varphi(k-t)$, as $a$ varies, traverses a translate of the full image of $\varphi$ and therefore includes a translate of the net in <a href="#schedule:final-net" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="schedule:final-net">[schedule:final-net]</a>.
  - Explanation: Marker absence supplies cuts with a fixed prefix monoid value whose clock values traverse the clock image.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-schedule-margins)): The choices above place every bounded inner window after $b'_{g'}$, every bounded end window after every outer cut, and all these windows strictly inside every complete episode. These assertions hold simultaneously for all the origin displacements and additional shifts just specified.
  - Explanation: Successively chosen margins keep all prescribed bounded inspections inside every complete episode.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-episode-prefix-code)): The language $E$ is a prefix code of nonempty words.
  - Explanation: The deterministic episode end rule prevents proper prefix pairs and excludes empty episodes.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-episode-shared-end)): Let a word in $E^*$ have first episode beginning at $s$, sampling origin $t=s+L$, endpoint $b$, and clock anchor $q$. Let $k=t+a$ be any genuine outer cut, with $a\in\mathcal H$. If a suffix starting at $k$ satisfies the common end guard, then its consumed endpoint is exactly $b$ and the clock anchor it uses is exactly $q$.* *Conversely, the actual suffix of that episode from $k$ to $b$ satisfies the guard whenever both end searches at $t$ are unchanged under every shift divisible by $B$ with absolute value at most $w$.
  - Explanation: A common end guard forces independently proposed suffix data to use the actual endpoint and clock anchor.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-outer-split)): For the total updates $F_e$ above and $D\subseteq E$ from <a href="#outer:exceptional-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="outer:exceptional-domain">[outer:exceptional-domain]</a>, the languages $P_x,Q_y$ have the following properties.* 1. *On any word in $E^*$, a match of $P_xQ_y$ beginning at the beginning of the word consumes exactly its first episode $e$ and satisfies $F_e(x)=y$.* 2. *For every $e\in E\setminus D$ and every $x\in V$, some cut of $e$ gives a match of $P_xQ_{F_e(x)}$.* *There is no match on the empty word.
  - Explanation: The outer split transmits the deterministic affine update on every successful pair and is complete outside the intrinsic exceptional domain.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-decode-anchor-list)): For a fixed actual episode, the values $Q_\tau$ in <a href="#decode:anchors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="decode:anchors">[decode:anchors]</a>, over all $p,p'\in\mathcal P$, belong to a set $\mathcal Q$ of at most $20$ positions.
  - Explanation: End marker separation and a common mismatch bound the finite list of candidate clock anchors.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-decode-sparsity)): For each complete episode and the positions $Q_\tau$ in <a href="#decode:anchors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="decode:anchors">[decode:anchors]</a>, $$\#\bigl\{(p,p')\in\mathcal P^2:p\ne p',\ Z(t+p-p')\ \text{or}\ A_*(t+p-p',Q_{t+p-p'})\bigr\}<\mu. \label{decode:whole-stencil-bound}$$ This bound includes every ordered distinct-slot pair, independently of the kinds assigned to its slots.
  - Explanation: Clock, width and separation estimates bound false origins across all ordered distinct stencil slots without restricting metadata kinds.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-decode-decoder)): Suppose the suffix test just described is paired with a genuine stencil prefix $[s,k)$, $k=s+p$, of a first episode $e\in D$ in a word of $D^*$. If the suffix test succeeds, it decodes the actual slot $p$ and consumes exactly to the actual endpoint $b$. Conversely, on the actual suffixes of a fixed episode $e\in D$, fewer than $\mu$ actual slots fail the decoder. In particular, each kind has a copy at which decoding succeeds.
  - Explanation: The true witness survives before uniqueness is imposed; successful decoding consumes the actual episode and leaves a usable copy of every kind.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-inner-split)): For the updates $G_e$ on $D$, the languages $\widetilde P_x,\widetilde Q_y$ satisfy:* 1. *on a word in $D^*$, every match of $\widetilde P_x\widetilde Q_y$ from its beginning consumes exactly the first episode $e$ and has $G_e(x)=y$;* 2. *every $e\in D$ admits such a split for every input $x\in U$.* *Thus this split has empty skip domain.
  - Explanation: Total hypothetical monoid tables and a coordinate certificate produce a sound and complete inner split with empty skip domain.
- **C19** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-compile-templates)): Fix a finite alphabet $\Sigma$, a finite monoid $M$, and a morphism $T:\Sigma^*\to M$. Consider finitely many templates $$\label{compile:eq-template} a c^i b,\qquad i\ge0,$$ where $a,b,c\in\Sigma^*$ are fixed for each template and $c\ne\varepsilon$. On each template suppose acceptance is determined by a fixed finite calculation using the following data:* 1. *letters in finitely many bounded windows at fixed integer offsets from the argument’s beginning or end;* 2. *order and availability of positions selected from fixed finite sets of such offsets, with unavailable required data causing rejection;* 3. *monoid products on available ordered intervals between these positions, and lengths or differences modulo fixed positive integers;* 4. *agreement of an interval between these positions with one of finitely many fixed periodic continuations, together with any required mismatch letter at its endpoint.* *Finite choices, universal tests, and uniqueness tests on fixed finite lists are allowed in the calculation. Then the accepted words have a generalized expression of height at most one over $\Sigma$. The same conclusion holds after adding finitely many exceptional words.
  - Explanation: Bounded calculations on periodic templates give ultimately periodic exponent sets and expressions with a single star layer.
- **C20** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-compile-components)): The episode language $E$, its skipped sublanguage $D$, and every suffix language in the outer and inner splits have generalized star height at most one over $\Sigma$. Every prefix language in these splits has height zero.
  - Explanation: Exact end checks give height bounds for standalone suffixes; fixed prefix lengths make prefix languages finite.
- **C21** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-compile-remainder)): For every $m_0\in M$, the language $N_{\rm end}\cap T^{-1}(m_0)$ has height at most one over $\Sigma$. Every word has a unique factorization into complete episodes followed by a word in $N_{\rm end}$.
  - Explanation: Every word factors uniquely into complete episodes and an incomplete remainder whose monoid fibers have the required height bound.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Generalized regular expression** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *generalized regular expression* over $\Sigma$ uses $0$, $1$, the singleton letters of $\Sigma$, union, concatenation, complement, and Kleene star.
- **Generalized star height** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a regular language $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$, write $h_\Sigma(L)$ for the least height of an expression defining $L$.
- **Prefix code** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A *prefix code* is a set $E\subseteq\Sigma^+$ containing no proper-prefix pair.
- **Graph language** ([source](#pm-h-8)): The *graph language* from $x$ to $y$ on $D^*$ is $$\{w\in D^*:G_w(x)=y\}.$$
- **Final remainder language** ([source](#pm-h-48)): Define the final remainder language $$N_{\rm end}=\neg(E\,\neg0).$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-compile-remainder-2): Finite recognizing morphism and episode factorization. -> Complement permits a universal upper bound on nesting for every regular language over its original finite alphabet.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-alg-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-split): Boundary induction through the split expression. -> Independent prefix and suffix tests propagate deterministic episode updates when soundness, exact consumption and completeness outside the skipped domain hold.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-alg-affine-recovery) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-affine-recovery): Subtract the output at zero by finite Boolean recovery. -> Outputs on the same word at the input and at zero recover the product of linear parts by finite Boolean operations.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift): Homogeneous linear lift of affine maps. -> An additional homogeneous coordinate turns affine composition into linear composition in reading order.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-alg-shift-table) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-shift-table): Finite dependency avoidance with exponentially decaying failure probability. -> A correction fixed independently of the input allows a tuple and input to select a coordinate line with constant corrected output.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-clock-robust) / [proof](#proof-stmt-clock-robust): Coordinate separation, majority conditions and dedicated forest coordinates. -> A finite torus clock limits independently chosen unequal tags and bounds exceptional roster pairs uniformly under translation and small perturbations.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-marker-local) / [proof](#proof-stmt-marker-local): Finite priority construction on nonperiodic blocks. -> A local translation invariant marker rule separates markers and forces short periodic blocks in markerless neighborhoods.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-marker-periodic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-marker-periodic): Common multiple periodicity and overlapping blocks. -> Sufficient overlap identifies periodic continuations and joins markerless blocks into a common periodic run.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-schedule-inner-cuts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-schedule-inner-cuts): Fixed schedule inequalities and reading-side separation. -> Fixed cut schedules put earlier searches on the prefix side and later searches on the suffix side under every prescribed hypothesis.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-schedule-periodic-cuts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-schedule-periodic-cuts): Eventual periodicity of monoid powers and coprime clock increments. -> Marker absence supplies cuts with a fixed prefix monoid value whose clock values traverse the clock image.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-schedule-margins) / [proof](#proof-stmt-schedule-margins): Successive lower bounds for inspection margins. -> Successively chosen margins keep all prescribed bounded inspections inside every complete episode.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-episode-prefix-code) / [proof](#proof-stmt-episode-prefix-code): Locality and deterministic first mismatch. -> The deterministic episode end rule prevents proper prefix pairs and excludes empty episodes.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-episode-shared-end) / [proof](#proof-stmt-episode-shared-end): Common search returns and overlapping periodic seeds. -> A common end guard forces independently proposed suffix data to use the actual endpoint and clock anchor.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-outer-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-outer-split): Clock alternatives, universal table certificates and periodic cuts. -> The outer split transmits the deterministic affine update on every successful pair and is complete outside the intrinsic exceptional domain.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-decode-anchor-list) / [proof](#proof-stmt-decode-anchor-list): Separated end markers and a shared mismatch. -> End marker separation and a common mismatch bound the finite list of candidate clock anchors.
- **R16** [claim](#stmt-decode-sparsity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-decode-sparsity): Uniform clock counts and separated search endpoints. -> Clock, width and separation estimates bound false origins across all ordered distinct stencil slots without restricting metadata kinds.
- **R17** [claim](#stmt-decode-decoder) / [proof](#proof-stmt-decode-decoder): Preserve the actual exceptional witness, then impose uniqueness and exact end. -> The true witness survives before uniqueness is imposed; successful decoding consumes the actual episode and leaves a usable copy of every kind.
- **R18** [claim](#stmt-inner-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inner-split): Compare every hypothetical table entry and certify the omitted product. -> Total hypothetical monoid tables and a coordinate certificate produce a sound and complete inner split with empty skip domain.
- **R19** [claim](#stmt-compile-templates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-compile-templates): Ultimately periodic arrays of template acceptance data. -> Bounded calculations on periodic templates give ultimately periodic exponent sets and expressions with a single star layer.
- **R20** [claim](#stmt-compile-components) / [proof](#proof-stmt-compile-components): Exact end template covers for standalone component languages. -> Exact end checks give height bounds for standalone suffixes; fixed prefix lengths make prefix languages finite.
- **R21** [claim](#stmt-compile-remainder) / [proof](#proof-stmt-compile-remainder): Template compilation of remainder fibers and greedy unique factorization. -> Every word factors uniquely into complete episodes and an incomplete remainder whose monoid fibers have the required height bound.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-recovery**: Outputs on the same word at the input and at zero determine the product of the linear parts by subtraction.
- **I-lift**: Products of homogeneous lifts follow the affine composites in reading order, without requiring invertibility.
- **I-height**: Regular languages admit the stated generalized star-height bound with complement in the original free monoid.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-regular**: Apply the bound to structured outputs with unrestricted nesting. -> The accepted language may fail to be regular.
- **A-cost**: Treat the nesting bound as a practical size or runtime guarantee. -> The construction can have large language-dependent finite parameters.
- **A-word**: Recover a linear output from affine evaluations on independently selected words. -> The translations need not coincide, so subtraction need not recover the desired linear part.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. Boolean affine recovery and homogeneous lifting give finite constructive computations. This kernel implements those algebraic interfaces; the complete language-expression construction remains outside its coverage.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batches of sequences of binary scalar affine maps [l,b].
# PRECONDITIONS: Each coefficient is an integer in {0,1}; sequence length at most 3; batch size at most 64.
# OUTPUTS: JSON per sequence: homogeneous outputs ordered by (v,c), and recovered linear outputs for v=0,1.
# TIME: Linear in the total number of maps and sequences.
# SPACE: Linear in the batch size for the output; constant working space per sequence.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact fmpz arithmetic reduced modulo 2.
# FAILURE: Invalid shape, nonbinary coefficients, or exceeded bounds raise ValueError.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
batch = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(batch, list) or len(batch) > 64:
    raise ValueError('invalid batch')
result = []
for seq in batch:
    if not isinstance(seq, list) or len(seq) > 3:
        raise ValueError('invalid sequence')
    alpha, beta = fmpz(1), fmpz(0)
    for pair in seq:
        if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2 or any(type(z) is not int or z not in (0,1) for z in pair):
            raise ValueError('invalid affine map')
        l, b = map(fmpz, pair)
        beta = (beta*l+b) % 2
        alpha = (alpha*l) % 2
    lift = [[int((v*alpha+c*beta) % 2), c] for v in range(2) for c in range(2)]
    recovered = [int(((v*alpha+beta)-beta) % 2) for v in range(2)]
    result.append({'lift':lift, 'recovered':recovered})
print(json.dumps(result, separators=(',',':')))
```

        "mutation": "Apply the affine translation on the homogeneous zero slice.",
        "observed_exit": 6,
        "receipt_sha256": "ef95001e51049767a95ab5ab34f3cf020cc8bc29cdb890db83dd529cab93bd59"
      }
    ],
    "oracle_code": "import json, sys\nfrom itertools import product\nsequences = json.load(open(sys.argv[1]))\ntruth = []\nfor updates in sequences:\n    table = []\n    for initial, flag in product((0,1), repeat=2):\n        state = initial\n        for scale, shift in updates:\n            state = (state & scale) ^ (flag & shift)\n        table.append([state, flag])\n    origin = table[1][0]\n    linear = [table[2*x+1][0] ^ origin for x in (0,1)]\n    truth.append(dict(lift=table, recovered=linear))\ntry:\n    observed = json.load(open(sys.argv[2]))\n    claimed = json.loads(sys.argv[3])\nexcept (ValueError, OSError):\n    sys.exit(7)\nsys.exit(0 if observed == truth == claimed else 6)\n",
    "output_contract": "Exact homogeneous composite outputs and the recovered linear outputs in the declared input order.",
    "reason": "Boolean affine recovery and homogeneous lifting give finite constructive computations. This kernel implements those algebraic interfaces; the complete language-expression construction remains outside its coverage.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON batches of sequences of binary scalar affine maps [l,b].\n# PRECONDITIONS: Each coefficient is an integer in {0,1}; sequence length at most 3; batch size at most 64.\n# OUTPUTS: JSON per sequence: homogeneous outputs ordered by (v,c), and recovered linear outputs for v=0,1.\n# TIME: Linear in the total number of maps and sequences.\n# SPACE: Linear in the batch size for the output; constant working space per sequence.\n# ARITHMETIC: Exact fmpz arithmetic reduced modulo 2.\n# FAILURE: Invalid shape, nonbinary coefficients, or exceeded bounds raise ValueError.\nimport json, sys\nfrom flint import fmpz\nbatch = json.load(sys.stdin)\nif not isinstance(batch, list) or len(batch) > 64:\n    raise ValueError('invalid batch')\nresult = []\nfor seq in batch:\n    if not isinstance(seq, list) or len(seq) > 3:\n        raise ValueError('invalid sequence')\n    alpha, beta = fmpz(1), fmpz(0)\n    for pair in seq:\n        if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2 or any(type(z) is not int or z not in (0,1) for z in pair):\n            raise ValueError('invalid affine map')\n        l, b = map(fmpz, pair)\n        beta = (beta*l+b) % 2\n        alpha = (alpha*l) % 2\n    lift = [[int((v*alpha+c*beta) % 2), c] for v in range(2) for c in range(2)]\n    recovered = [int(((v*alpha+beta)-beta) % 2) for v in range(2)]\n    result.append({'lift':lift, 'recovered':recovered})\nprint(json.dumps(result, separators=(',',':')))\n",

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Regular lexical specifications and finite-state validation rules can be expressed with bounded generalized star nesting. The transfer requires a finite alphabet and a regular accepted language; it provides a representational guarantee rather than a performance guarantee. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | A finite abstraction of legal event traces can use the regular-language bound. The result applies to the trace abstraction only; it does not establish properties of continuous dynamics or unbounded game state. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A validator or constrained-output tool can use the bound when its accepted strings are regular over a finite alphabet. Exact language equivalence and the ambient complement domain remain implementation obligations. |
| algorithms | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The split identity, affine recovery and homogeneous lift provide interfaces for deterministic computations on prefix-code episode sequences. A proposed implementation must retain total updates, exact episode consumption and the declared skipped domain. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The construction uses finite tables indexed by every monoid value, including hypothetical prefix products that no actual prefix realizes. Such tables offer an adaptable representation for exact finite-state computations, without a storage bound. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem bounds generalized star nesting of regular-language expressions. It supplies no accelerator architecture, throughput bound or hardware resource bound. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The language-representation theorem supplies no cryptographic hardness, secrecy or adversarial security claim. A regular-language encoding alone cannot supply those guarantees. |
| machine_learning | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | A constrained decoder may use the result for a fixed regular set of allowed outputs over a finite alphabet. The guarantee concerns the allowed language and does not establish learning quality or decoding efficiency. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction proves an upper bound on expression nesting without an expression-size or running-time bound. It supplies no objective-function improvement, optimisation solver guarantee or scheduling approximation guarantee. |
| distributed_systems | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Finite-state message or conversation traces can be represented as regular languages, so the bound transfers to that representation. The paper does not establish distributed consensus, fault tolerance or execution liveness. |
| other (Formal languages and automata) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | The direct application is formal-language theory: every regular language over its own finite alphabet has generalized star height at most three, with complement in the same free monoid. The theorem does not decide whether one always suffices. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The universal bound requires a finite alphabet and a regular language. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to infinite alphabets or nonregular languages.']
- **L2** (formal_scope): Complement must use the original ambient free monoid. Blocked use: ['Changing the complement universe without proving language preservation.']
- **L3** (unknown): Expression size and running time are not bounded by the construction. Blocked use: ['Inferring compact expressions or faster evaluation from bounded nesting.']
- **L4** (counterexample_scope): The result leaves open whether generalized height one always suffices. Blocked use: ['Treating the upper bound as a matching lower bound.']
- **L5** (formal_scope): Ordinary star-height lower bounds do not automatically transfer to expressions allowing complement. Blocked use: ['Applying ordinary lower bounds as generalized lower bounds.']
- **L6** (hypothesis): Split propagation requires prefix-code episodes and deterministic total updates. Blocked use: ['Using arbitrary word pieces or partial, choice-dependent updates without proving split obligations.']
- **L7** (hypothesis): Affine recovery compares both computations on the same word. Blocked use: ['Subtracting outputs from different episode sequences.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The omitted coordinate may depend on the input state. Blocked use: ['Assuming a single coordinate works for every input.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): Missing required windows cause rejection, rather than removal of candidates. Blocked use: ['Dropping unavailable origins from universal or uniqueness tests.']
- **L10** (hypothesis): The decoder verifies the exact end at the decoded origin in both branches. Blocked use: ['Omitting endpoint verification when all larger searches are present.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): The suffix table retains all hypothetical monoid inputs. Blocked use: ['Restricting table comparisons to prefix products realized by observed words.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): Finite-state decidability alone does not establish the component height bound. Blocked use: ['Replacing the periodic-template argument with a regularity check alone.']
- **L13** (edge_case): The empty alphabet is treated separately by height-zero expressions. Blocked use: ['Assuming a nonempty episode exists for an empty input.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 134 (`lean/docs/134.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Finite Monoid Computations and a Uniform Generalized Star-Height Bound](../../preprints/Finite-Monoid-Computations-and-a-Uniform-Generalized-Star-Height-Bound-September-25-2026/Finite-Monoid-Computations-and-a-Uniform-Generalized-Star-Height-Bound-September-25-2026.pdf)
> - [Generalized Star Height at Most Four](../../preprints/Generalized-Star-Height-at-Most-Four-September-25-2026/Generalized-Star-Height-at-Most-Four-September-25-2026.pdf)
> - [Generalized Star Height at Most Three](../../preprints/Generalized-Star-Height-at-Most-Three-September-25-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Generalized star height measures the nesting of Kleene stars in regular expressions that also allow Boolean operations. The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized expression over the same alphabet of star height at most three. This is stronger than the bound of thirteen in the accompanying finite-monoid paper. The selected statement asserts the uniform expression bound, without separately encoding every construction step of that paper.
>
> The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression over that same alphabet with star height at most three. Generalized expressions allow Boolean operations as well as concatenation and Kleene star. The bound of three implies the accompanying paper's bound of four; the selected statement concerns the expression bound itself.
>
> Generalized star height measures the nesting of Kleene stars in regular expressions that also allow Boolean operations. The formalization proves that every regular language over a finite alphabet has a generalized regular expression over that same alphabet of star height at most three. Complements are taken in the same free monoid. This is the paper's uniform bound.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `Expression` | inductive |
| `language` | def |
| `height` | def |
| `HasHeightAtMost` | def |
| `main` | theorem |

Comparator source `GeneralizedStarHeight.lean` (1368 bytes, sha256 `795709e34771f1c786ecae7c123e8a586c426d8e93c8b5bc82f2904d5340556e`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace GeneralizedStarHeight

universe u

inductive Expression (Alphabet : Type u) : Type u
  | zero : Expression Alphabet
  | one : Expression Alphabet
  | letter : Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | union : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | concat : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | compl : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet
  | star : Expression Alphabet → Expression Alphabet

namespace Expression

open scoped Computability

def language {Alphabet : Type u} : Expression Alphabet → Language Alphabet
  | .zero => 0
  | .one => 1
  | .letter a => {[a]}
  | .union P Q => P.language + Q.language
  | .concat P Q => P.language * Q.language
  | .compl P => P.languageᶜ
  | .star P => P.language∗

def height {Alphabet : Type u} : Expression Alphabet → ℕ
  | .zero | .one | .letter _ => 0
  | .union P Q | .concat P Q => max P.height Q.height
  | .compl P => P.height
  | .star P => 1 + P.height

end Expression

def HasHeightAtMost {Alphabet : Type u} (L : Language Alphabet) (n : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ P : Expression Alphabet, P.language = L ∧ P.height ≤ n

theorem main {Alphabet : Type u} [Finite Alphabet] (L : Language Alphabet) (hL : L.IsRegular) :
    HasHeightAtMost L 3 := by
  sorry

end GeneralizedStarHeight

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every regular language over a finite alphabet has generalized star height at most three, with complement taken in the same free monoid. We express finite-monoid computations using a prefix code of word pieces.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The star operation permits a finite language expression to describe an arbitrarily long repetition. Its nesting depth measures how many layers of repetition an expression uses. Once complement is allowed, Boolean conditions can replace some of those layers. The question studied here is whether a fixed number of layers suffices for every regular language, independently of its alphabet and recognizing automaton.

Fix a finite alphabet $\Sigma$. A *generalized regular expression* over $\Sigma$ uses $0$, $1$, the singleton letters of $\Sigma$, union, concatenation, complement, and Kleene star. The constants denote $\emptyset$ and $\{\varepsilon\}$, and complement is always taken in $\Sigma^*$. Its height is determined by $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{ht}(0)=\operatorname{ht}(1)=\operatorname{ht}(a)&=0,\\
 \operatorname{ht}(P\cup Q)=\operatorname{ht}(PQ)&=\max\{\operatorname{ht}(P),\operatorname{ht}(Q)\},\\
 \operatorname{ht}(\neg P)&=\operatorname{ht}(P),&
 \operatorname{ht}(P^*)&=1+\operatorname{ht}(P).
\end{aligned}$$ For a regular language $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$, write $h_\Sigma(L)$ for the least height of an expression defining $L$. In particular, complement preserves the nesting already present in its argument. Finite Boolean combinations preserve a common height bound, and the full language $\Sigma^*=\neg0$ has height zero.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ and every regular language $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$, $$h_\Sigma(L)\le3.$$*

</div>

The expressions in the theorem use the letters of $\Sigma$ itself. Their finite parameters and their lengths may depend on the language. The construction gives an upper bound on nesting, without a bound on expression size or running time.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and its mathematical context

The corresponding *ordinary* star-height problem omits complement. Eggan connected ordinary star height with the cycle structure of transition graphs, and Dejean and Schützenberger established unbounded ordinary star height already over a two-letter alphabet (Eggan 1963; Dejean and Schützenberger 1966) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Eggan1963](#ref-Eggan1963), [DejeanSchutzenberger1966](#ref-DejeanSchutzenberger1966)]. These results concern the operations allowed in ordinary expressions. Their lower bounds do not carry over merely by adding complement.

Generalized height zero already has a different character: its languages are the *star-free* languages, which need not be finite. A finite monoid $M$ recognizes a language when membership depends only on the image of a morphism from $\Sigma^*$ to $M$. Schützenberger characterized the star-free languages as exactly those recognized by finite aperiodic monoids (Schützenberger 1965) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Schutzenberger1965](#ref-Schutzenberger1965)]. Here a monoid is aperiodic if every element $m$ satisfies $m^r=m^{r+1}$ for some $r\ge1$. A simple language requiring one star is $(aa)^*$ over $\{a\}$: its displayed expression has height one, while every star-free unary language is finite or cofinite, as induction under union, complement, and concatenation shows.

The algebraic approach has also produced substantial families of height at most one. Pin, Straubing, and Thérien proved this bound for languages recognized by finite nilpotent groups of class two, and for further classes of finite monoids (Pin, Straubing, and Thérien 1992, Theorems 7.3 and 7.8) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[PST1992](#ref-PST1992)]. Another line of results concerns words in which a fixed nonempty factor occurs contiguously a prescribed number of times modulo an integer $n\ge2$, with overlapping occurrences counted. Bourne and Ruškuc proved height-one bounds for factors of length at most three (Tom Bourne and Ruškuc 2016, Proposition 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BourneRuskuc2016](#ref-BourneRuskuc2016)]; Bourne subsequently extended the result to arbitrary fixed factors in his thesis (Thomas Bourne 2017, Theorem 2.25) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Bourne2017](#ref-Bourne2017)]. These results explain how complement can simplify nontrivial counting languages, but their restricted families do not supply a bound for arbitrary regular languages.

A universal finite upper bound and a universal upper bound of one are different assertions; Straubing distinguishes them explicitly in his account of the problem (Straubing 2002, 537) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Straubing2002](#ref-Straubing2002)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a positive answer to the boundedness question. It does not exhibit a language of generalized height greater than one, and does not decide whether one always suffices.

Two companion constructions explain the setting of this proof. The paper *Finite Monoid Computations and a Uniform Generalized Star-Height Bound* proves a bound of thirteen by finite computations, a prediction argument, and a decision tree that retains the complete earlier history (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1 and Sections 2–7) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HeightThirteen](#ref-HeightThirteen)]. *Generalized Star Height at Most Four* proves a bound of four using constancy on affine coordinate lines, several marker scales, and product transport across moving periodic boundaries (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 1.1 and Sections 2–6) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HeightFour](#ref-HeightFour)]. The present construction obtains three through two split steps. Each of the three papers gives its own complete proof; the numerical theorem of one is not used to prove another. The prediction and multiscale methods remain different constructions even though the bound here is smaller.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Computing a word value with independent tests

Every regular language has a recognizing morphism $T:\Sigma^*\to M$ into a finite monoid. We therefore seek expressions for the fibers of $T$. The construction parses a word into complete pieces called *episodes*, followed by a final remainder. The episode language $E$ is a prefix code, meaning that no member is a proper prefix of another. This makes the episode boundaries unique.

An episode will act on a finite vector space by an affine map. To propagate its state, we cut the episode and test the prefix for an input state and the suffix for an output state. The tests are languages placed in separate factors of a concatenation. Thus their auxiliary choices are independent. A valid split has to meet two separate obligations: every successful pair beginning at a true episode boundary must consume that entire first episode and transmit its update; for every input state, a successful pair must exist on each episode that is not assigned to a specified skipped class.

Section <a href="#alg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:section">2</a> proves a split identity that converts these obligations into expressions for arbitrary episode sequences. If the individual tests and the episode domain have height at most one and sequence computations on the skipped class have height at most $H$, the identity gives height at most $\max\{2,H+1\}$. The split and affine recovery methods are developed in the two companions (OpenAI 2026a, Lemmas 2.2–2.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HeightThirteen](#ref-HeightThirteen)] and (OpenAI 2026b, Lemmas 2.1–2.2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[HeightFour](#ref-HeightFour)]; all versions used here are proved locally. An affine correction does not destroy the original computation: the difference between the outputs on $x$ and on zero recovers the linear part on $x$. Finite unions and intersections express this subtraction without another star.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Why two splits suffice

The first split uses later cuts in an episode and a finite set of *tags*. A tag is a tuple of states, interval products, and indices that a test proposes to use. The prefix and suffix choose their tags separately. A clock restricts the pairs they can jointly realize: either they agree, or there is only one possible unequal pair. In the second case we define the episode’s affine translation so that this one pair transmits the prescribed input to the prescribed output. To use the clock, however, both tests must calculate the same total time. A separate end guard establishes that fact; it is not a consequence of the clock.

The clock also needs room to realize its permitted choices at actual cuts. Outside an exceptional set of total times, every tag permits a whole neighborhood of compatible prefix times, with a common positive margin. The clock also supplies a finite list of candidate times for which the number of exceptional pairs is bounded uniformly under translation and sufficiently small simultaneous perturbations. These are the additional clock properties used here; Section <a href="#clock:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clock:section">3</a> compares the quantitative bounds.

When tags agree, the first split has two ways to work. If all the marker boundaries are present, their successive intervals give a tuple of monoid products. A finite shift table makes one coordinate of this tuple irrelevant to the transmitted output. The prefix checks the earlier products, the suffix checks the later products, and a finite certificate checks every possible value of the product crossing their cut. If a marker boundary is absent, a long periodic stretch instead allows the cut to move through all clock residues while keeping its prefix product fixed. This argument uses eventual periodicity of powers in a finite monoid; it requires no cancellation.

The shift table is uniform in a stronger sense than a product-action statement. One tuple length, chosen using only the finite entry alphabet and state space, must work for every table from tuples to linear maps. A single correction table is chosen for each such map, independently of the input vector. The coordinate that can be omitted may depend on the tuple and vector. This quantifier order is proved by a finite avoidance argument of the local-lemma type of Erdős and Lovász (Erdős and Lovász 1975) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ErdosLovasz1975](#ref-ErdosLovasz1975)].

The clock and marker conditions used to guarantee the first split’s completeness can fail on an intrinsic sublanguage $D\subseteq E$. The second split handles every episode in $D$, with no further skipped class. A suffix at an earlier cut can use the failed condition as a witness to the actual origin. Those early cuts form a fixed finite set, or *stencil*, containing many copies of every required tuple of metadata. The differences between distinct stencil positions have different residues. Marker searches with widths indexed by these residues make false witnesses sparse over all ordered pairs of positions, including pairs with incorrect metadata.

End detection needs special care in this count. A smaller bounded search fixes an episode’s end when it finds an end marker; otherwise a periodic seed is followed to its first mismatch. A larger bounded search supplies the clock anchor whenever possible. If that larger search is absent at one nearby proposed origin, all the smaller searches are absent, and their overlapping seeds have one common mismatch. If every larger search is present, all candidate clock anchors are already bounded data. In either case the true origin’s witness remains available before the suffix requires uniqueness. After identifying it, the suffix must separately check its exact endpoint. Counting over the whole stencil leaves a usable copy of every metadata tuple and supplies the second split.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### From finite tests to height-three expressions

The local marker construction is a finite-window instance of the marker-versus-periodicity method (Krieger 1982, Lemma 2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Krieger1982](#ref-Krieger1982)]; its full priority construction and periodic-overlap argument are given in Section <a href="#marker:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="marker:section">4</a>. Section <a href="#clock:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clock:section">3</a> proves the robust clock. Section <a href="#schedule:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="schedule:section">5</a> then chooses all finite parameters in dependency order. In particular, a provisional rational clock fixes the early stencil before the marker scale is chosen. A later rational approximation preserves those fixed trials and makes the final clock period coprime to all relevant short periods.

Sections <a href="#episode:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="episode:section">6</a> and <a href="#outer:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="outer:section">7</a> construct episodes, force a common end under independently chosen roles, and prove the first split. Section <a href="#decode:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="decode:section">8</a> establishes the bound on all false origins and the suffix decoder. Section <a href="#inner:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inner:section">9</a> uses the remaining suffix to compute a total table indexed by every hypothetical prefix product in $M$, including values that no prefix realizes. Applying the shift-table lemma to this table gives the second split.

The last step, Section <a href="#sec:compilation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compilation">10</a>, controls expression height for each component on its own domain. A standalone suffix, even with false origin data, has its own exact-end test. It consequently lies in a finite union of fixed-word templates $ac^ib$. The accepted exponents are ultimately periodic, which gives height-one expressions over the original letters. The incomplete remainder has the same kind of expression. The inner split starts from empty skip computations and gives height two; recovery makes those computations the skips for the outer split, giving height three. Finite Boolean recovery and concatenation with the remainder add no star. Tags, states, and residues serve only as finite indices in these expressions.

<a id="alg:section"></a>
### Finite computations and split expressions

The language problem will be represented by a finite computation. This section supplies three interfaces for manipulating it: an expression identity that propagates an update through episodes, a Boolean operation that removes an affine correction, and a finite table that allows one unknown coordinate. Each interface will be used twice, with different state spaces. The related split and recovery constructions in (OpenAI 2026a; 2026b, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[HeightThirteen](#ref-HeightThirteen), [HeightFour](#ref-HeightFour)] explain the method; the exact statements and proofs needed here are included below.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Monoid values and deterministic updates

If $\Sigma=\emptyset$, its only languages are $0$ and $1$, both of height zero. Assume henceforth that $\Sigma$ is nonempty, and fix a morphism $$T:\Sigma^*\longrightarrow M$$ into a finite monoid $M$. For a deterministic finite automaton, take $M$ to be the finite monoid of state transformations induced by words, including the identity transformation. Its accepted language is a finite union of fibers of $T$. Thus it suffices to construct a height-three expression for each fiber of every such morphism.

Letters occupy intervals $[a,a+1)$ with integer endpoints. For an available interval $[a,b)$, $a\le b$, write $T_{a,b}$ for its monoid value, with $T_{a,a}=1_M$. Products are in reading order: $$T_{a,c}=T_{a,b}T_{b,c}.$$ Let $V=\mathbf F_2^M$ have basis $(e_m)_{m\in M}$. Each $d\in M$ acts linearly on the right by $e_m d=e_{md}$. This is the linearized right regular action (Almeida et al. 2009, sec. 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[AMSV2009](#ref-AMSV2009)]. It is faithful because $e_{1_M}d=e_d$ identifies $d$. Vectors are written on the left of their linear maps, so $vLA$ means first apply $L$, then $A$. We will also use $U=V\oplus\mathbf F_2$; the additional coordinate will turn affine maps on $V$ into linear maps on $U$. All vector spaces below are finite.

Put $N_M=|M|!$. Finiteness gives <a id="alg:power-periodicity"></a>$$\label{alg:power-periodicity}
          c^{i+N_M}=c^i\qquad(c\in M,\ i\ge |M|).$$ Indeed two of $c^0,\ldots,c^{|M|}$ agree, say $c^a=c^b$ with $0\le a<b\le |M|$. Powers are then periodic from exponent $a$, with period $b-a$, which divides $N_M$. This includes $|M|=1$.

A *prefix code* is a set $E\subseteq\Sigma^+$ containing no proper-prefix pair. Its members will be called *episodes*. Every word in $E^*$ has a unique factorization into episodes: the first factors of two proposed factorizations are comparable by the prefix relation and therefore equal, after which induction applies. The empty word has the empty factorization.

For $D\subseteq E$, assign to each $e\in D$ a deterministic total map $G_e:S\to S$ on a fixed finite state set $S$. On $w=e_1\cdots e_r$, let $G_w$ apply the updates in reading order; $G_\varepsilon$ is the identity. The *graph language* from $x$ to $y$ on $D^*$ is $$\{w\in D^*:G_w(x)=y\}.$$ The indicated domain is part of this definition. The update is determined by the episode itself, independently of choices made by an expression that tests its graph.

Finite intersections can be expressed using union and complement and do not increase generalized star height. A finite language has height zero: write its words as concatenations of letters, use $1$ for $\varepsilon$, and take a finite union. Auxiliary finite indices below are never input letters.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The split identity

The next lemma separates two obligations. Every successful split must transmit the correct state and consume exactly one episode. Existence of a split is required only outside the domain that is to be skipped.

<a id="stmt-alg-split"></a>
<div id="alg:split" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Split identity). *Let $D'\subseteq D\subseteq E$, where $E$ is a prefix code, and give each $e\in D$ a deterministic total update $G_e$ of a finite set $S$. Suppose languages $P_x,Q_y\subseteq\Sigma^*$, $x,y\in S$, satisfy:*

1.  *If $w\in D^*$ has a prefix $uv$ with $u\in P_x$ and $v\in Q_y$, then $w$ has a first episode $e$, $uv=e$, and $G_e(x)=y$.*

2.  *For every $e\in D\setminus D'$ and every $x\in S$, $e\in P_xQ_{G_e(x)}$.*

<a id="alg:split-formula"></a>
*Let $W_{x,y}$ be the graph languages for these same updates on $(D')^*$. Then the graph language from $x$ to $y$ on $D^*$ is $$\label{alg:split-formula}
\begin{split}
D^*\cap\Bigg[W_{x,y}\ \cup\ &
 \left(\bigcup_{i\in S}W_{x,i}P_i\right)
 \left(\bigcup_{j,i\in S}Q_jW_{j,i}P_i\right)^*\\[-2pt]
 &\hspace{24mm}\cdot
 \left(\bigcup_{l\in S}Q_lW_{l,y}\right)\Bigg].
\end{split}$$ If $D$, all $P_x$, and all $Q_y$ have height at most one, while all $W_{x,y}$ have height at most $H\ge0$, the resulting graph languages have height at most $\max\{2,1+H\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-split"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-split</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a word in the right-hand side. Its membership in $D^*$ fixes the ambient episode factorization, but does not by itself align an arbitrary interior factor with that factorization. We establish alignment in order, starting at the beginning of the word.

The first $W$-factor begins at a true boundary. Since its words are in $(D')^*$, its own first episode and the ambient first episode are prefix comparable. Both belong to the prefix code $E$, so they are equal. Repeating this argument shows that the whole $W$-factor consumes complete ambient episodes, and its endpoint is another true boundary. Its graph indices describe their actual composite update.

Now consider an accepted factorization through the second term of <a href="#alg:split-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:split-formula">[alg:split-formula]</a>. The concatenation of the first $P$-factor and the following $Q$-factor begins at the true boundary just established. Condition <span class="upright">(i)</span> says that their concatenation is exactly the next episode and takes the indicated input state to the indicated output. The next $W$-factor starts at that episode’s true endpoint, so the same prefix-code argument applies again. Alternating these two steps through the factorization establishes every boundary and propagates every state. Only this induction licenses alignment; no assertion about a general interior factor in $E^*$ is needed. A zero-fold middle star leaves one $P,Q$ pair, to which the same argument applies. The first term $W_{x,y}$ already records the desired composite on its domain.

For the reverse inclusion, follow the actual computation on a word of $D^*$. Use condition <span class="upright">(ii)</span> to split each episode outside $D'$, and group every intervening run of episodes from $D'$ into a $W$-factor with its actual endpoint states. These choices form the second term in <a href="#alg:split-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:split-formula">[alg:split-formula]</a>. If there is no episode outside $D'$, the word belongs to $W_{x,y}$. This includes the empty word. Empty domains are therefore allowed; if $S$ is empty there are no indexed assertions.

The height estimate can be read directly from the expression. The factor $D^*$ has height at most two. Each factor inside the middle star has height at most $\max\{1,H\}$, and starring it gives at most $1+\max\{1,H\}$. Unions, concatenations, and the outer intersection preserve the maximum of their argument heights. Thus the bound is $\max\{2,1+H\}$. ◻

</details>

When $D'=\emptyset$, the skip language $W_{x,y}$ is $1$ for $x=y$ and $0$ otherwise. Its height is zero, so this case of the lemma has height bound two. Notice also that condition <span class="upright">(i)</span> rules out a successful pair on the empty word. Independent choices in $P_x$ and $Q_y$ must satisfy it even when their proposed metadata disagree.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Removing affine corrections

We will deliberately alter an episode’s translation term to make its split possible. The following identity explains why this is harmless. The output on zero isolates the translation of a whole composite, even when none of its linear factors is invertible. The two computations must be required on the same word, which is why the expression uses an intersection.

<a id="stmt-alg-affine-recovery"></a>
<div id="alg:affine-recovery" class="lemma">

<a id="alg:linear-recovery-formula"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Boolean affine recovery). *Let $S$ be a finite vector space and let the episode updates on $D\subseteq E$ be $$G_e(v)=vL_e+b_e,$$ with $L_e\in\operatorname{End}_{\rm lin}(S)$ and $b_e\in S$ determined by $e$. If $R_{x,z}$ are the graph languages of their composites on $D^*$, then the graph language for the product of their linear parts, from $x$ to $y$, is $$\label{alg:linear-recovery-formula}
                  \bigcup_{b\in S}
                         \bigl(R_{x,y+b}\cap R_{0,b}\bigr).$$ A common height bound for the $R_{x,z}$ is preserved.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-affine-recovery"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-affine-recovery</summary>

*Proof.* Composition in reading order obeys $(vL+b)A+c=vLA+(bA+c)$. Induction, starting from the identity on the empty word, therefore writes each word’s composite as $v\mapsto vL+b$, where $L$ is precisely the product of its episode linear parts. For that word, the output at zero is $b$, and the output at $x$ is $y+b$ exactly when $xL=y$. Requiring both facts on the word gives the intersection in <a href="#alg:linear-recovery-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:linear-recovery-formula">[alg:linear-recovery-formula]</a>; taking the union allows every possible translation $b$. These are finite Boolean operations, so they preserve the asserted height bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift"></a>
<div id="alg:homogeneous-lift" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Homogeneous lift). *For a finite vector space $S$ over a finite field $\mathbf F$, the map $F(v)=vL+b$ has the linear lift on $S\oplus\mathbf F$ <a id="alg:lift-formula"></a>$$\label{alg:lift-formula}
                     \widehat F(v,c)=(vL+cb,c).$$ Products of these lifts are the lifts of the corresponding composites in reading order. In particular, graph languages for products of the lifts give those of the affine composites by using inputs $(v,1)$ and outputs $(y,1)$, without increasing height.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-homogeneous-lift</summary>

*Proof.* The displayed map is linear in $(v,c)$. For $F(v)=vL+b$ followed by $G(v)=vA+d$, the two lifted maps give $$(v,c)\longmapsto(vLA+c(bA+d),c),$$ which is the lift of their affine composite. Induction proves the product assertion; the slice $c=1$ proves the graph assertion. The empty product is the identity in both spaces. No invertibility is required. We will use this with $S=V$, $\mathbf F=\mathbf F_2$, and $S\oplus\mathbf F=U$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A shift table that hides one entry

A cut can leave one interval product unreadable by either factor. We will replace the demand to know that product by a finite certificate that checks all of its possible values. To make such a certificate available, we need a deterministic affine correction with a constant coordinate line through every tuple, for every input state.

The order of quantifiers matters: the length is selected first, uniformly in the table of linear maps; a correction is then fixed for each table; only afterward may a tuple and input state select a successful coordinate. The product-action version in (OpenAI 2026b, Lemma 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[HeightFour](#ref-HeightFour)] motivates this step, but an arbitrary table is needed for the second split below.

<a id="stmt-alg-shift-table"></a>
<div id="alg:shift-table" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Shift table). *For every nonempty finite set $A$ and every finite vector space $S$ there is an integer $l\ge1$, depending only on $|A|$ and $|S|$, with the following property. For every function $$f:A^l\longrightarrow
                                    \operatorname{End}_{\rm lin}(S)$$ there is a function $\beta_f:A^l\to S$ such that, for every $\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_l)\in A^l$ and $x\in S$, there is an $i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}$ for which <a id="alg:shift-constancy"></a>$$\label{alg:shift-constancy}
 b\longmapsto x f(a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1},b,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_l)
       +\beta_f(a_1,\ldots,a_{i-1},b,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_l)$$ is constant on $A$. The integer $l$ is uniform over $f$, and $\beta_f$ is independent of $x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-shift-table"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-shift-table</summary>

*Proof.* Put $a=|A|$ and $q=|S|$. If $a=1$ or $q=1$, take $l=1$ and $\beta_f=0$; every required function is constant. Assume $a,q>1$. For an integer $l$ to be chosen, choose independent uniform vectors $\beta(\mathbf c)\in S$ at all cells $\mathbf c\in A^l$. Fix any table $f$. For each $x\in S$ and $\mathbf a\in A^l$, let $\mathcal B_{x,\mathbf a}$ be the event that none of the $l$ coordinate lines through $\mathbf a$ has the asserted constancy.

Condition on the value of $\beta(\mathbf a)$. For a specified line, constancy forces exactly one value of $\beta$ at each of its other $a-1$ cells, regardless of $f$. The line therefore succeeds with probability $q^{1-a}$. Different lines share only their central cell, so these successes are conditionally independent. The conditional failure probability is independent of the central value, giving $$\Pr(\mathcal B_{x,\mathbf a})
                          =(1-q^{1-a})^l=:p_l.$$

This event depends only on random values at Hamming distance at most one from $\mathbf a$, where Hamming distance counts unequal entries. Events with centers at distance greater than two use disjoint random variables. Hence each event is independent of the joint family of all such distant events. A dependency neighborhood has size at most <a id="alg:dependency-bound"></a>$$\label{alg:dependency-bound}
 q\left(1+l(a-1)+\binom l2(a-1)^2\right)-1
              \ \le\ D_l:=q(1+la+l^2a^2).$$ The factor $q$ includes every possible input vector at each center.

We use the following form of the finite dependency argument of Erdős and Lovász (Erdős and Lovász 1975, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ErdosLovasz1975](#ref-ErdosLovasz1975)] and include its proof. Suppose each event of a finite family is independent of the joint family of its nonneighbors, has at most $D$ neighbors, and has probability at most $s(1-s)^D$, where $0<s<1$. Then the probability that all the events fail to occur is positive. Here is the conditional-probability argument, to make the precise independence requirement explicit.

Induct on the size of a set of other events being conditioned not to occur. Along with positivity of these conditioning events, prove that the conditional probability of any remaining event is at most $s$. For an empty set this follows from the assumed bound. Positivity for a set of size $k$ follows by deleting one event and using the bound for a conditioning set of size $k-1$. To prove the conditional bound at size $k$, split the conditioning set into the neighbors $J_1$ and nonneighbors $J_0$ of the event $B$ under consideration. If $J_1$ is empty, joint independence gives its unconditional probability. Otherwise, writing $C_i$ for simultaneous nonoccurrence of $J_i$, we have $$\Pr(B\mid C_0\cap C_1)
 \le \frac{\Pr(B\mid C_0)}{\Pr(C_1\mid C_0)}
 \le \frac{\Pr(B)}{(1-s)^{|J_1|}}
 \le s.$$ For the denominator, expose the events in $J_1$ one at a time; every conditioning set then has size at most $k-1$, so the induction bounds each nonoccurrence probability below by $1-s$. The numerator uses joint independence from $J_0$. This completes the induction, and successive conditioning gives positive probability of full avoidance.

Apply this estimate with $D=D_l$ and $s=1/(2(D_l+1))$. Bernoulli’s inequality gives $$(1-s)^{D_l}\ge1-D_ls\ge\tfrac12,
 \qquad s(1-s)^{D_l}\ge\frac1{4(D_l+1)}.$$ The probability $p_l$ decays exponentially in $l$, whereas $D_l$ grows quadratically. Choose $l$ so that $p_l\le1/(4(D_l+1))$. This choice depends only on $a,q$, and works for every table $f$. Avoidance of all $\mathcal B_{x,\mathbf a}$ supplies the required $\beta_f$ simultaneously for all cells and input vectors. ◻

</details>

Fix one successful $\beta_f$ for each of the finitely many possible functions $f$ at these dimensions. The resulting map $x\mapsto xf(\mathbf a)+\beta_f(\mathbf a)$ is now determined by the tuple and table, before any splitting choices are made. A prefix can certify its output using the other entries by checking every replacement for the omitted coordinate. The lemma promises a usable coordinate for each actual input, not one coordinate usable for all inputs at once. Nor does it restrict $f$ to tables obtained from feasible word prefixes. This unrestricted table domain will be essential when a suffix computes hypothetical prefix values in Section <a href="#inner:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inner:section">9</a>.

<a id="clock:section"></a>
### A robust tag clock

The shift table supplies compatible data when both factors use the same tag. The next construction limits the damage from independent tag choices. For each possible total time, it will permit only equal tags or exactly one unequal ordered pair. Later, the episode’s affine shift will accommodate that one exceptional pair.

We need more than this compatibility rule. A finite roster of candidate positions must encounter few exceptional totals, uniformly under every translation. Outside the exceptional set, each tag must admit a decomposition with a common positive interior margin, so a sufficiently fine grid can find one. The logarithmic estimate and its forest argument are developed in (OpenAI 2026a, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[HeightThirteen](#ref-HeightThirteen)], with constant $1{,}300{,}000$. Here the estimate $10^7\log(2m)$ accompanies a torus construction whose common interior margin and perturbation tolerance let us postpone the final clock period.

Write $\mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ and $\|x\|_{\mathbb T}=\min_{j\in\mathbb Z}|x-j|$. A finite product $\mathcal T=\mathbb T^C$ is given the maximum metric $\operatorname{dist}(u,v)=\max_{c\in C}\|u_c-v_c\|_{\mathbb T}$. Addition and subtraction are coordinatewise. For subsets, $I+J$ denotes the set of sums of their elements, and $z-J=\{z-j:j\in J\}$. All logarithms in the clock estimate are natural.

<a id="stmt-clock-robust"></a>
<div id="clock:robust" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Robust tag clock). *Let $\Lambda$ and $\mathcal P$ be finite nonempty sets, with $m=|\Lambda|$. There exist a finite-dimensional torus $\mathcal T$, sets $I_\alpha,J_\alpha\subseteq\mathcal T$ for $\alpha\in\Lambda$, a set $W\subseteq\mathcal T$, points $v_p\in\mathcal T$ for $p\in\mathcal P$, and constants $\rho,\eta>0$ with these properties:*

1.  *For each $z\in\mathcal T$, the edge set <a id="clock:edge-set"></a>$$\label{clock:edge-set}
     \mathcal E_z=\{(\alpha,\lambda)\in\Lambda^2:
                                      z\in I_\alpha+J_\lambda\}$$ is either contained in the diagonal or consists of one off-diagonal pair.*

2.  *If $z\notin W$, then for every $\alpha\in\Lambda$, $I_\alpha\cap(z-J_\alpha)$ contains a ball of radius $\rho$. In particular $\mathcal E_z$ is the full diagonal.*

<a id="clock:roster-estimate"></a>
3.  *For every family $(\tilde v_p)_{p\in\mathcal P}$ satisfying $\operatorname{dist}(\tilde v_p,v_p)<\eta$ for all $p$, and every $a\in\mathcal T$, $$\label{clock:roster-estimate}
     \#\{(p,p')\in\mathcal P^2:p\ne p',\quad
                   a+\tilde v_{p'}-\tilde v_p\in W\}
                             \le c_{\rm cl}\log(2m),
     \qquad c_{\rm cl}=10^7.$$*

*The constants $\rho,\eta$ and the dimension may depend on both finite sets, but $c_{\rm cl}$ is absolute.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clock-robust"><summary>Proof of stmt-clock-robust</summary>

*Proof.* The proof separates compatibility from counting. First coordinates will exclude all competitors of an unequal edge except its reversal. Type batches will exclude the reversal too. Each block also permits a diagonal decomposition away from an explicitly specified obstruction. Finally we assign the roster values so that, at any translation, very few ordered pairs can meet the union of those obstructions.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Partitions of the tags.

Put $$a_0=\lceil64\log(2m)\rceil,\qquad
        b_0=\lceil\log_2m\rceil,\qquad F=400.$$ Choose $a_0$ left/right partitions of $\Lambda$ with the following property: whenever $\alpha\ne\lambda$ and $\{\alpha,\lambda\}\ne\{\gamma,\nu\}$, some partition places $\alpha$ on the left, $\lambda$ on the right, and $\gamma,\nu$ on the same side. This includes $\gamma=\nu$.

Assign each tag independently to either side, with equal probabilities. A constraint is feasible even when some displayed tags coincide: the excluded set equality ensures that putting $\gamma$ and $\nu$ together does not force $\alpha$ and $\lambda$ together. At most four tag choices therefore suffice to satisfy it, giving probability at least $1/16$. There are no more than $m^4$ constraints. With $a_0$ independent partitions, the expected number left unsatisfied is bounded by $$m^4(15/16)^{a_0}
          \le m^4e^{-a_0/16}\le m^4(2m)^{-4}=1/16<1.$$ Some choice therefore misses none. Give each partition one circle coordinate, called a first coordinate.

Also choose $b_0$ partitions into Types 1 and 2 that separate every two distinct tags, by assigning distinct binary strings of length $b_0$ to the tags. Each such partition receives a batch of coordinates. For every ordered list of $F$ directed pairs $(p,p')$ of distinct roster slots whose underlying unordered edges are distinct and form a forest, include a dedicated coordinate in this batch. The forest restriction will allow arbitrary edge differences to be realized by successive assignments of the roster values. Add spare coordinates if necessary so that the batch has positive odd size. There are finitely many lists, and a one-coordinate spare batch is allowed if no list exists. These first coordinates and batches form the finite coordinate set $C$ of $\mathcal T$. For $m=1$, there are no Type partitions, but $a_0\ge1$ still supplies first coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### First coordinates and their roster values.

Enumerate $\mathcal P=\{p_0,\ldots,p_{r-1}\}$. In each first coordinate choose $v_{p_j}=\theta2^j\pmod1$, where $\theta>0$ is small enough that all the values lie in an arc of length less than $1/8$. The ordered differences for distinct slots are nonzero and pairwise distinct on the circle. Indeed a positive integer difference $2^j-2^i=2^i(2^{j-i}-1)$ determines $i$ by its power of two, then $j$ by its odd factor; the sign distinguishes the reverse orientation. The small scale prevents collisions modulo one.

Since this is finite data, choose $0<\epsilon<1/100$ so small that these nonzero differences are separated from one another and from zero by more than $12\epsilon$, with a strict margin. If there are no distinct-slot pairs, any sufficiently small positive $\epsilon$ works. In the first coordinate for a left/right partition, impose the requirements $$\begin{array}{c|cc}
 \text{side of }\alpha & I_\alpha & J_\alpha\\ \hline
 \text{left}  & \|i\|_{\mathbb T}\le\epsilon
               & \|j\|_{\mathbb T}\ge4\epsilon\\
 \text{right} & \|i\|_{\mathbb T}\ge4\epsilon
               & \|j\|_{\mathbb T}\le\epsilon.
\end{array}$$ An edge from a left tag to a right tag forces its total to have distance at most $2\epsilon$ from zero. A same-side edge has one near and one far summand, so its total has distance at least $3\epsilon$ from zero. These possibilities are disjoint.

Every total $z$ with $\|z\|_{\mathbb T}>6\epsilon$ has a diagonal decomposition in this coordinate, by assigning zero to the near summand and $z$ to the far summand. Perturbing the first summand by distance at most $\epsilon/2$, while perturbing the second by the opposite amount, preserves both requirements. Thus the diagonal decomposition has a uniform interior margin.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### Type 1 batches.

Let $$C_I=\{0,1,3\}/6,\qquad C_J=\{2,4,5\}/6
                 \quad\text{in }\mathbb T.$$ For a Type 1 tag, every coordinate of its $I$-vector must be within $1/16$ of $C_I$, and every coordinate of its $J$-vector within $1/16$ of $C_J$. Their center sums contain all six multiples of $1/6$. For an arbitrary total $z$ in one coordinate, choose a center sum $c_I+c_J$ with a signed error $\delta$ of magnitude at most $1/12$. The decomposition $$z=(c_I+\delta/2)+(c_J+\delta/2)$$ puts each summand within $1/24$ of its center, leaving margin $1/48$ inside the allowed radius. It therefore remains valid when the first summand is moved by at most $1/96$ and the second by the opposite amount. These choices are independent in the batch coordinates.

<a id="clock:type-one-difference"></a>
On the other hand, the center sets have circular distance at least $1/6$. Every difference of allowed Type 1 vectors satisfies $$\label{clock:type-one-difference}
              \|i_c-j_c\|_{\mathbb T}
                 \ge 1/6-2/16=1/24
                 \quad\text{in every batch coordinate }c.$$

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### Type 2 batches.

In a batch of $2s+1$ coordinates, a Type 2 tag requires, separately for each of its $I$- and $J$-vectors, at least $s+1$ coordinates at distance at most $1/100$ from zero. Suppose a total $z$ has one coordinate $c_0$ at distance at most $1/200$ from zero. Set the first summand to $z_{c_0}$ and the second to zero there. Partition the other $2s$ coordinates into two groups of size $s$. In one group set the first summand to zero, and in the other set the second summand to zero; in each coordinate the remaining summand is determined by $z$. Each vector has $s+1$ designated small coordinates. At every such coordinate the margin to the threshold $1/100$ is at least $1/200$, so moving the first vector by at most $1/400$ in the maximum metric and the second oppositely preserves both majority conditions.

<a id="clock:type-two-difference"></a>
Conversely, the small-coordinate sets of two allowed Type 2 vectors intersect. Their difference has at least one coordinate satisfying $$\label{clock:type-two-difference}
                            \|i_c-j_c\|_{\mathbb T}\le2/100.$$ This includes $s=0$, a one-coordinate batch.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### The graph and the interior condition.

Define $I_\alpha$ and $J_\alpha$ by all their coordinate requirements. If an off-diagonal edge $\alpha\to\lambda$ occurs, a separating left/right partition excludes every competitor $(\gamma,\nu)$ with $\{\gamma,\nu\}\ne\{\alpha,\lambda\}$. Only its reversal could remain. If both orientations occurred, there would be suitable elements of their four sets with $$i_\alpha+j_\lambda=i_\lambda+j_\alpha,
 \qquad i_\alpha-j_\alpha=i_\lambda-j_\lambda.$$ A Type partition separating $\alpha$ and $\lambda$ makes the common difference satisfy both <a href="#clock:type-one-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clock:type-one-difference">[clock:type-one-difference]</a> and <a href="#clock:type-two-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clock:type-two-difference">[clock:type-two-difference]</a>, contrary to $2/100<1/24$. This proves <span class="upright">(i)</span>.

Let $W$ be the union of these obstructions for a total $z$:

1.  some first coordinate has distance at most $6\epsilon$ from zero;

2.  some Type batch has no coordinate at distance at most $1/200$ from zero.

If $z\notin W$, all the diagonal constructions above apply. They combine across disjoint coordinate blocks. For example, the common positive radius <a id="clock:interior-radius"></a>$$\label{clock:interior-radius}
                  \rho=\min\{\epsilon/2,1/96,1/400\}$$ gives a ball in $I_\alpha\cap(z-J_\alpha)$ for every $\alpha$. Indeed a perturbation of the first vector in this ball produces the opposite perturbation of the second, and both remain allowed in every block. This proves the interior assertion. Since $\Lambda$ is nonempty, the graph alternative now makes the graph outside $W$ exactly the full diagonal.
